<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove that one independent sample per element suffices for a constant-competitive prophet inequality on every finite matroid. The guarantee holds even when the arrival-order adversary observes all samples, all online values, and the algorithm's entire random seed. The rule needs no description of the value distributions and achieves the absolute competitive ratio $`2^{-310}`$.

- **C1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There is a measurable online selection rule, independent of the value distributions, with the following property. Let $M=(E,\mathcal I)$ be any finite known labeled matroid, and let $(S_e,V_e:e\in E)$ be mutually independent nonnegative real random variables, with $S_e$ and $V_e$ having the same arbitrary law $D_e$. Assume $\mathbb E[\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_M(V)]<\infty$. The rule receives exactly the sample vector $S$ before arrivals and uses a seed $R$ independent of $S,V$.* *For every measurable permutation rule $\pi$ depending on $M$, the laws $(D_e)$, $S$, $V$, and $R$, the rule accepts an independent set $A$, irrevocably deciding at each arrival using only the available history, and $$\label{eq:main} \mathbb E\left[\sum_{e\in A}V_e\right] \ge 2^{-310}\mathbb E[\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_M(V)].$$
  - Explanation: An independent sample per label supports a constant expected reward guarantee under complete-seed adversarial ordering.
- **C2** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-hidden)): There is a measurable rule in the fixed-vector formulation that is feasible for every realization and every permutation, and for every $w\in[0,\infty)^E$ satisfies $$\label{eq:hidden-guarantee} \mathbb E_R\left[\min_{\pi}\sum_{e\in A(w,R,\pi)}w_e\right] \ge 2^{-293}\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_M(w).$$ Its revealed mask is selected before any weights are seen.
  - Explanation: The fixed-weight rule controls the expectation of the minimum reward over orders after its seed is exposed.
- **C3** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-simulation)): A measurable fixed-vector rule satisfying <a href="#eq:hidden-guarantee" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hidden-guarantee">[eq:hidden-guarantee]</a> yields a measurable one-sample rule with the same competitive ratio against the adversary of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.
  - Explanation: Sample and value copies are glued coordinatewise to preserve the fixed-vector guarantee against the stated adversary.
- **C4** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-filter)): The set $Y$ is independent, $Y\subseteq U$, and $$\label{eq:candidate-mass} \mathbb E_H u(Y)=W/2,\qquad \mathbb E_H u(U)\le W.$$
  - Explanation: The candidate filter preserves an independent benchmark while controlling expected total candidate weight.
- **C5** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-density)): The largest maximizer in <a href="#eq:density-objective" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:density-objective">[eq:density-objective]</a> exists, the map $Q_h$ is increasing, and $P\subseteq Q_h(P)$. If $Q=Q_h(P)$, then for every set $Z$, $$\label{eq:density-residual} \|D_h\cap(\mathop{\mathrm{cl}}(Q+Z)\setminus Q)\|\le\kappa\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(Z\mid Q).$$
  - Explanation: Density optimization has a largest monotone maximizing flat and bounds additional sampled labels by conditional rank.
- **C6** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-paths)): The paths increase with $k$ and satisfy $$\label{eq:path-inclusions} F_{h-1}(k)\subseteq X_h(k)\subseteq F_h(k)\subseteq X_h(k+1).$$ They equal $E$ for $k\ge0$. Moreover $X_h(k)=L$ for $k<a_h$ and $F_h(k)=L$ for $k<a_h-1$. If $e$ is a nonloop, its nominal birth $$b_h(e)=\min\{k:e\in X_h(k)\}$$ is either $a_h$, with $e\in Q_h(L)$, or belongs to $[a_h+2,0]$.
  - Explanation: Nested nominal and guarded paths have a controlled activation schedule and exclude a birth immediately after activation.
- **C7** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-feasibility)): The main rule maintains an independent accepted set at every arrival prefix, for every permutation. It is a measurable rule based only on the allowed information.
  - Explanation: Layerwise greedy choices remain independent under every interleaving and use only the permitted information.
- **C8** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-generators)): For each listed group $h$ there is a set $J_h\subseteq D_h$ and increasing subsets $J_h(k)\subseteq J_h$ such that $$\label{eq:generator-budget} X_h(k)=\mathop{\mathrm{cl}}(F_{h-1}(k)+J_h(k)),\qquad \|J_h\|\le \|D_h\|/\kappa.$$ In particular $\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(Q_h(L))\le \|D_h\|/\kappa$.
  - Explanation: A common generator set controls every nominal expansion along a group path.
- **C9** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-transactions)): Let $V_0$ be finite, let its bits be independent Bernoulli random variables, and let $\mathcal A\subseteq2^{V_0}$ be increasing with failure probability $\delta>0$. An adaptive procedure queries each coordinate at most once and divides its queries into transactions. The next query, transaction boundaries, and stopping decisions depend only on the transcript and, if desired, an independent auxiliary seed. Suppose the number of transactions has a deterministic finite bound. A transaction may be declared successful only if the positive bits queried within that transaction alone form a certificate for $\mathcal A$. Then $$\label{eq:certificate-budget} \mathbb E[\text{number of successful transactions}] \le\log(1/\delta).$$ Here and below $\log$ denotes the natural logarithm.
  - Explanation: Adaptive transactions certifying an increasing event consume a bounded logarithmic resource.
- **C10** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-exit)): Every nonloop $d$ exits its $h$-th nominal path by the end of the reverse computation, and $$\label{eq:low-exit-hazard} \mathbb P(\text{$d$ exits at a step with }p_{b-1}<\eta\mid H,D)<\tfrac12.$$ Consequently, $$\label{eq:squared-exit-mass} \sum_{b=a_h}^{0} \mathbb E\bigl[\mathbf 1_{\{d\in X_h(b)\}}p_{b-1}^{2}\mid H,D\bigr] \ge\frac{\eta}{2}.$$
  - Explanation: Reverse exposure prevents most exits from occurring only at small predictable exit probabilities.
- **C11** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-safe)): For every listed group $h$, $$\label{eq:safe-samples} \mathbb E[\sigma_h\mid H,D] \ge\frac{\eta}{4}\|D_h\cap Y\|-\frac{\|D_h\|}{\kappa}.$$
  - Explanation: The safety count retains sampled independent mass up to a density-generator loss.
- **C12** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-transfer)): Fix $H$. For a true group $U_i$, define $z_i=z_h$ and $\sigma_i=\sigma_h$ if the group is listed with index $h$, and set both quantities to zero if it is unlisted. If $n_i\ge\kappa$, then $$\label{eq:rank-transfer} \mathbb E[z_i\mid H] \ge\frac{\gamma\|Y_i\|-2^{-23}n_i}{\kappa}, \qquad \gamma=\frac\eta{16}=2^{-16}.$$
  - Explanation: Safe sampled mass transfers to nominal unobserved rank for sufficiently large true groups.
- **C13** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-pivot-patterns)): Let $M$ be any finite matroid, and let $V$ be a set of $n$ nonloops. Let $s\ge0$ be an integer. Fix an ordered sequence of labeled *old occurrences*, each with a mark in $\{0,1\}$, whose labels span $E$. Also fix at most $s$ labeled *movable occurrences*. Repetitions of labels are allowed in both sequences. Insert the movable occurrences into the old sequence in any order and positions, and give each movable occurrence either mark. For $f\in V$, its pivot is the first occurrence after whose insertion $f$ is spanned; record that occurrence’s mark. The number of resulting vectors of $n$ marks is at most $$\label{eq:pivot-pattern-bound} 4^s\sum_{\ell=0}^{s}\binom n\ell,$$ where $\binom n\ell=0$ for $\ell>n$.
  - Explanation: Marked-pivot patterns admit a bound independent of the number of old occurrences.
- **C14** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-pattern-family)): Fix $H$ and a true group $U_i$ of size $n=n_i\ge\kappa$. Condition also on the parity and on the $D,C,T$ bits outside $U_i$, but do not condition on their bits in $U_i$ or on whether $U_i$ is listed. There is a deterministic family $\mathfrak R_i\subseteq 2^{U_i}$ with $$\label{eq:pattern-family-size} \|\mathfrak R_i\|\le \exp\left(\frac{1000\log\kappa}{\kappa}\,n\right)$$ such that, whenever this group is listed and $z_h\le n/\kappa$, every residual <a href="#eq:residual-set" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:residual-set">[eq:residual-set]</a> obtained from witnesses <a href="#eq:rank-witnesses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rank-witnesses">[eq:rank-witnesses]</a> belongs to $\mathfrak R_i$.
  - Explanation: A family fixed before focal bits are exposed contains every relevant small-rank residual.
- **C15** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-all-orders)): For each realization of all coins and every permutation $\pi$, $$\label{eq:all-orders} N_{\ge h}(\pi)\ge\lambda_h.$$
  - Explanation: The order-independent final-layer rank lower-bounds accepted cumulative counts for every permutation.
- **C16** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-rank-cost)): For each listed group $h$, the nominal statistic $z_h$ satisfies $$\label{eq:rank-cost} \sum_b\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(S_{h,b}\mid\widehat F(b-2)+K_{h,b})\ge z_h-c_h, \qquad c_h=\|C_h\|+\sum_{j>h}\bigl(\|C_j\|+\|D_j\|/\kappa\bigr).$$
  - Explanation: A common generator budget pays for final-path rank losses across the entire timeline.
- **C17** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-thinning)): For a listed group $h$, let $\mathcal F_{-h}$ be generated by $H,D,C$, the parity $\varepsilon$, and all test-mask memberships outside $G_h$. Then $$\label{eq:test-thinning} \mathbb E[\lambda_h\mid\mathcal F_{-h}] \ge t\sum_b\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(S_{h,b}\mid\widehat F(b-2)+K_{h,b}) \ge t(z_h-c_h).$$
  - Explanation: Independent eligibility thinning preserves expected conditional rank.
- **C18** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-secretary)): For each known finite labeled matroid $M=(E,\mathcal I)$, there is one measurable randomized online rule $\mathcal S_M$ with the following property. Fix any vector $w\in[0,\infty)^E$ of finite nonnegative weights before all rule and arrival randomness. Without knowing $w$, the rule draws its complete initial seed $Q$, independently of a uniformly random permutation $\sigma$ of $E$, and fixes a possibly random prefix length $K$ before any arrival. It observes the first $K$ label–weight pairs of $\sigma$ in order and rejects those labels.* *After this ordered prefix is observed and before any suffix arrival, an adversary may know $M$, the entire fixed vector $w$, the ordered prefix, and the complete seed $Q$, including $K$, the branch choice, and unused coins. It may choose any measurable permutation $\tau$ of the remaining labels as a function of this information. The rule is not told the future suffix order and decides irrevocably at each suffix arrival while maintaining an independent set. Writing $A_{\mathcal S}$ for its accepted set, for every such adversary, $$\label{eq:secretary-guarantee} \mathbb E\left[\sum_{e\in A_{\mathcal S}}w_e\right] \ge 2^{-293}\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_M(w).$$ The expectation is over the rule seed and random prefix, and over any adversary randomization. The adversary cannot alter the random-prefix law or choose the weights after observing the seed or prefix.
  - Explanation: A random rejected prefix realizes the sacrificed mask and supports adversarial full-seed suffix ordering.

          "anchor": "stmt-thm-main",
          "exact_quote": "There is a measurable online selection rule, independent of the value distributions, with the following property. Let $M=(E,\\mathcal I)$ be any finite known labeled matroid, and let $(S_e,V_e:e\\in E)$ be mutually independent nonnegative real random variables, with $S_e$ and $V_e$ having the same arbitrary law $D_e$. Assume $\\mathbb E[\\mathop{\\mathrm{OPT}}_M(V)]<\\infty$. The rule receives exactly the sample vector $S$ before arrivals and uses a seed $R$ independent of $S,V$.*\n\n*For every measurable permutation rule $\\pi$ depending on $M$, the laws $(D_e)$, $S$, $V$, and $R$, the rule accepts an independent set $A$, irrevocably deciding at each arrival using only the available history, and $$\\label{eq:main}\n \\mathbb E\\left[\\sum_{e\\in A}V_e\\right]\n       \\ge 2^{-310}\\mathbb E[\\mathop{\\mathrm{OPT}}_M(V)].$$",
          "source_id": "PM-111.1"
        }
      ],

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-transactions",
        "exact_quote": "Let $V_0$ be finite, let its bits be independent Bernoulli random variables, and let $\\mathcal A\\subseteq2^{V_0}$ be increasing with failure probability $\\delta>0$. An adaptive procedure queries each coordinate at most once and divides its queries into transactions. The next query, transaction boundaries, and stopping decisions depend only on the transcript and, if desired, an independent auxiliary seed. Suppose the number of transactions has a deterministic finite bound. A transaction may be declared successful only if the positive bits queried within that transaction alone form a certificate for $\\mathcal A$. Then $$\\label{eq:certificate-budget}\n \\mathbb E[\\text{number of successful transactions}]\n       \\le\\log(1/\\delta).$$ Here and below $\\log$ denotes the natural logarithm.",
        "source_id": "PM-111.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {

        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-transactions",
          "exact_quote": "Let $V_0$ be finite, let its bits be independent Bernoulli random variables, and let $\\mathcal A\\subseteq2^{V_0}$ be increasing with failure probability $\\delta>0$. An adaptive procedure queries each coordinate at most once and divides its queries into transactions. The next query, transaction boundaries, and stopping decisions depend only on the transcript and, if desired, an independent auxiliary seed. Suppose the number of transactions has a deterministic finite bound. A transaction may be declared successful only if the positive bits queried within that transaction alone form a certificate for $\\mathcal A$. Then $$\\label{eq:certificate-budget}\n \\mathbb E[\\text{number of successful transactions}]\n       \\le\\log(1/\\delta).$$ Here and below $\\log$ denotes the natural logarithm.",
          "source_id": "PM-111.1"
        }
      ],
      "statement_anchor": "stmt-lem-transactions"
    },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-exit",
        "exact_quote": "Every nonloop $d$ exits its $h$-th nominal path by the end of the reverse computation, and $$\\label{eq:low-exit-hazard}\n \\mathbb P(\\text{$d$ exits at a step with }p_{b-1}<\\eta\\mid H,D)<\\tfrac12.$$ Consequently, $$\\label{eq:squared-exit-mass}\n \\sum_{b=a_h}^{0}\n  \\mathbb E\\bigl[\\mathbf 1_{\\{d\\in X_h(b)\\}}p_{b-1}^{2}\\mid H,D\\bigr]\n       \\ge\\frac{\\eta}{2}.$$",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-pivot-patterns",
        "exact_quote": "Let $M$ be any finite matroid, and let $V$ be a set of $n$ nonloops. Let $s\\ge0$ be an integer. Fix an ordered sequence of labeled *old occurrences*, each with a mark in $\\{0,1\\}$, whose labels span $E$. Also fix at most $s$ labeled *movable occurrences*. Repetitions of labels are allowed in both sequences. Insert the movable occurrences into the old sequence in any order and positions, and give each movable occurrence either mark. For $f\\in V$, its pivot is the first occurrence after whose insertion $f$ is spanned; record that occurrence’s mark. The number of resulting vectors of $n$ marks is at most $$\\label{eq:pivot-pattern-bound}\n 4^s\\sum_{\\ell=0}^{s}\\binom n\\ell,$$ where $\\binom n\\ell=0$ for $\\ell>n$.",
        "source_id": "PM-111.1"
      },

The number of resulting vectors of $n$ marks is at most $$\\label{eq:pivot-pattern-bound}\n 4^s\\sum_{\\ell=0}^{s}\\binom n\\ell,$$ where $\\binom n\\ell=0$ for $\\ell>n$.",

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-pattern-family",
        "exact_quote": "Fix $H$ and a true group $U_i$ of size $n=n_i\\ge\\kappa$. Then $$\\label{eq:test-thinning}\n \\mathbb E[\\lambda_h\\mid\\mathcal F_{-h}]\n \\ge t\\sum_b\\mathop{\\mathrm{r}}(S_{h,b}\\mid\\widehat F(b-2)+K_{h,b})\n \\ge t(z_h-c_h).$$",
          "source_id": "PM-111.1"
        }
      ],
      "statement_anchor": "stmt-lem-thinning"
    },
    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-cor-secretary",
        "exact_quote": "For each known finite labeled matroid $M=(E,\\mathcal I)$, there is one measurable randomized online rule $\\mathcal S_M$ with the following property. Fix any vector $w\\in[0,\\infty)^E$ of finite nonnegative weights before all rule and arrival randomness. Without knowing $w$, the rule draws its complete initial seed $Q$, independently of a uniformly random permutation $\\sigma$ of $E$, and fixes a possibly random prefix length $K$ before any arrival. It observes the first $K$ label–weight pairs of $\\sigma$ in order and rejects those labels.*\n\n\n*After this ordered prefix is observed and before any suffix arrival, an adversary may know $M$, the entire fixed vector $w$, the ordered prefix, and the complete seed $Q$, including $K$, the branch choice, and unused coins. It may choose any measurable permutation $\\tau$ of the remaining labels as a function of this information. The rule is not told the future suffix order and decides irrevocably at each suffix arrival while maintaining an independent set. Writing $A_{\\mathcal S}$ for its accepted set, for every such adversary, $$\\label{eq:secretary-guarantee}\n \\mathbb E\\left[\\sum_{e\\in A_{\\mathcal S}}w_e\\right]\n \\ge 2^{-293}\\mathop{\\mathrm{OPT}}_M(w).$$ The expectation is over the rule seed and random prefix, and over any adversary randomization. The adversary cannot alter the random-prefix law or choose the weights after observing the seed or prefix.",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Offline optimum** ([source](#pm-h-2)): For a nonnegative vector $v=(v_e:e\in E)$, write $$\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_M(v)=\max_{I\in\mathcal I}\sum_{e\in I}v_e.$$
- **Matroid rank and closure** ([source](#sec:setup)): For $X\subseteq E$, its rank $\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(X)$ is the maximum size of an independent subset of $X$, and $\mathop{\mathrm{cl}}(X)=\{e\in E:\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(X\cup\{e\})=\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(X)\}$ is its closure.
- **Flat and spanning** ([source](#sec:setup)): A flat equals its closure; $X$ spans $A$ when $A\subseteq\mathop{\mathrm{cl}}(X)$.
- **Conditional rank** ([source](#sec:setup)): Write $L=\mathop{\mathrm{cl}}(\varnothing)$ for the set of loops, and put $$\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(A\mid P)=\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(A\cup P)-\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(P),\qquad \mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_M(w)=\max_{I\in\mathcal I}\sum_{e\in I}w_e.$$
- **Independence over a base** ([source](#sec:setup)): A set is independent over $P$ if its conditional rank over $P$ equals its cardinality.
- **Rounded weights** ([source](#pm-h-8)): For $w_e>0$, round down to $u_e=B^{\lfloor\log_B w_e\rfloor}$, and put $u_e=0$ when $w_e=0$.
- **Density expansion** ([source](#pm-h-10)): For a listed group $h$ and a flat $P$, let $Q_h(P)$ be the largest flat maximizing $$\label{eq:density-objective} f_h(Z)=\|D_h\cap Z\|-\kappa\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(Z) \qquad\text{over flats }Z\supseteq P.$$
- **Increasing Boolean event** ([source](#pm-h-16)): An increasing Boolean event is a family $\mathcal A\subseteq2^V$ closed under taking supersets.
- **Positive certificate** ([source](#pm-h-16)): A positive certificate for $\mathcal A$ is a set $I$ with $I\in\mathcal A$: setting the coordinates in $I$ to one already forces the event, whatever the other bits are.

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-thinning-2): Combine the fixed-vector bound with the sample/value simulation. -> An independent sample per label supports a constant expected reward guarantee under complete-seed adversarial ordering.
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-prop-hidden) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-thinning-2): Mix the maximum branch and main branch, then account for geometric rounding. -> The fixed-weight rule controls the expectation of the minimum reward over orders after its seed is exposed.
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-lem-simulation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-simulation): Glue coordinates selected by the initial mask and integrate the pointwise order minimum. -> Sample and value copies are glued coordinatewise to preserve the fixed-vector guarantee against the stated adversary.
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-lem-filter) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-filter): Compare the candidate filter with priority greedy on the fair mask. -> The candidate filter preserves an independent benchmark while controlling expected total candidate weight.
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-lem-density) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-density): Apply supermodularity on the finite lattice of flats and compare a further extension. -> Density optimization has a largest monotone maximizing flat and bounds additional sampled labels by conditional rank.
- **R6** [claim](#stmt-lem-paths) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-paths): Induct over listed groups using monotone extensive maps and guard containment. -> Nested nominal and guarded paths have a controlled activation schedule and exclude a birth immediately after activation.
- **R7** [claim](#stmt-lem-feasibility) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-feasibility): Combine independent sets over successive nested layer bases and check measurability. -> Layerwise greedy choices remain independent under every interleaving and use only the permitted information.
- **R8** [claim](#stmt-lem-generators) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-generators): Select basis extensions and telescope the captured density-label counts. -> A common generator set controls every nominal expansion along a group path.
- **R9** [claim](#stmt-lem-transactions) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-transactions): Track the ratio of conditional failure probabilities and telescope logarithmic increments. -> Adaptive transactions certifying an increasing event consume a bounded logarithmic resource.
- **R10** [claim](#stmt-lem-exit) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-exit): Bound successful disjoint certificates in dyadic hazard bands. -> Reverse exposure prevents most exits from occurring only at small predictable exit probabilities.
- **R11** [claim](#stmt-lem-safe) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-safe): Couple the ordinary and hybrid transition, then apply independent parity. -> The safety count retains sampled independent mass up to a density-generator loss.
- **R12** [claim](#stmt-lem-transfer) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-pattern-family-2): Compare safe samples with witnessed rank and uniformly bound sacrificed residuals. -> Safe sampled mass transfers to nominal unobserved rank for sufficiently large true groups.
- **R13** [claim](#stmt-lem-pivot-patterns) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-pivot-patterns): Count possible retained greedy bases and strict affine sign patterns. -> Marked-pivot patterns admit a bound independent of the number of old occurrences.
- **R14** [claim](#stmt-lem-pattern-family) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-pattern-family): Encode birth validity and residual exclusion with marked spanning sequences. -> A family fixed before focal bits are exposed contains every relevant small-rank residual.
- **R15** [claim](#stmt-lem-all-orders) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-all-orders): Use layerwise greedy spanning and contract all lower-weight competitors. -> The order-independent final-layer rank lower-bounds accepted cumulative counts for every permutation.
- **R16** [claim](#stmt-lem-rank-cost) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-rank-cost): Bound every final expansion by a common generator set and telescope conditional ranks. -> A common generator budget pays for final-path rank losses across the entire timeline.
- **R17** [claim](#stmt-lem-thinning) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-thinning): Intersect fixed independent rank witnesses with the independent test mask. -> Independent eligibility thinning preserves expected conditional rank.
- **R18** [claim](#stmt-cor-secretary) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-secretary): Realize the product mask as a binomial random prefix and reconstruct the source seed. -> A random rejected prefix realizes the sacrificed mask and supports adversarial full-seed suffix ordering.

          "anchor": "stmt-lem-pivot-patterns",
          "exact_quote": "Let $M$ be any finite matroid, and let $V$ be a set of $n$ nonloops. Let $s\\ge0$ be an integer. Fix an ordered sequence of labeled *old occurrences*, each with a mark in $\\{0,1\\}$, whose labels span $E$. Also fix at most $s$ labeled *movable occurrences*. Repetitions of labels are allowed in both sequences. Insert the movable occurrences into the old sequence in any order and positions, and give each movable occurrence either mark. For $f\\in V$, its pivot is the first occurrence after whose insertion $f$ is spanned; record that occurrence’s mark. The number of resulting vectors of $n$ marks is at most $$\\label{eq:pivot-pattern-bound}\n 4^s\\sum_{\\ell=0}^{s}\\binom n\\ell,$$ where $\\binom n\\ell=0$ for $\\ell>n$.",

        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-pattern-family",

Without knowing $w$, the rule draws its complete initial seed $Q$, independently of a uniformly random permutation $\\sigma$ of $E$, and fixes a possibly random prefix length $K$ before any arrival. It observes the first $K$ label–weight pairs of $\\sigma$ in order and rejects those labels.*\n\n\n*After this ordered prefix is observed and before any suffix arrival, an adversary may know $M$, the entire fixed vector $w$, the ordered prefix, and the complete seed $Q$, including $K$, the branch choice, and unused coins. The rule is not told the future suffix order and decides irrevocably at each suffix arrival while maintaining an independent set. <a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **maximum**: The maximum branch lower-bounds its expected minimum reward by the largest rounded positive nonloop weight divided by 4.
- **feasibility**: Every arrival prefix has an independent accepted set.
- **sampling**: The sample and online vectors have mutually independent coordinates with matching laws at each label.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **runtime**: Deploy the full construction under a polynomial independence-query budget. -> The existence guarantee supplies no such computational budget.
- **dependent_samples**: Use correlated samples or samples whose laws differ from online values. -> The sample-to-value coupling requires the stated independence and matching laws.
- **nonmatroid**: Apply the guarantee to arbitrary feasibility constraints. -> The feasibility and rank comparisons require matroid structure.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The maximum rule is a finite constructive branch with a fair independent mask and an explicit minimum-over-orders reward bound. The demonstrator covers that branch; the main layered rule and stochastic theorem remain outside its scope.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON batch of finite labeled matroids and rounded integer weights.
# PRECONDITIONS: At most four labels; valid independence family; weights are zero or powers of B=2**32.
# OUTPUTS: Exact expected minimum-over-orders maximum-branch reward as numerator/denominator strings.
# TIME: Exponential mask enumeration times factorial arrival enumeration per instance.
# SPACE: Linear state per permutation, plus the finite input and output batches.
# ARITHMETIC: Exact integers and python-flint fmpq rational arithmetic.
# FAILURE: Inputs outside the contract are unsupported; no approximate certificate is returned.
import json, sys
from itertools import permutations
from flint import fmpq
batch = json.load(sys.stdin)
answers = []
for case in batch:
    w = case['weights']; n = len(w)
    ind = set(case['independent'])
    assert n <= 4 and 0 in ind
    live = [e for e in range(n) if (1 << e) in ind and w[e] > 0]
    key = lambda e: (w[e], -e)
    total = 0
    for mask in range(1 << n):
        seen = [e for e in live if mask & (1 << e)]
        threshold = max([key(e) for e in seen], default=(-1, 0))
        worst = None
        for order in permutations(range(n)):
            reward = 0
            for e in order:
                if e in live and not (mask & (1 << e)) and key(e) > threshold:
                    reward = w[e]
                    break
            worst = reward if worst is None else min(worst, reward)
        total += worst
    value = fmpq(total, 1 << n)
    answers.append(str(value.numerator) + '/' + str(value.denominator))
print(json.dumps(answers, separators=(',', ':')))
```

|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | A software selector can adapt the finite online rule when its known feasibility constraint is a matroid and its value observations obey the independent sample interface. No concrete deployment or computational-cost bound is provided. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT — the paper supplies no game mechanics, strategic-equilibrium model, or simulation contract; its adversary only selects an arrival permutation. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT — no standalone engineering tool, tool interface, or tool-performance guarantee is specified. |
| algorithms | True | strong | implementable | inferred | The paper specifies a finite measurable online selection algorithm with a constant reward guarantee against an ordering adversary observing samples, values, and the complete seed. It also transfers the fixed-vector guarantee to secretary selection with an adversarial suffix. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT — sets, flats, and rank tests describe mathematical state, but no data-structure design or operation-cost guarantee is supplied. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT — no accelerator architecture, hardware mapping, or hardware performance analysis is supplied. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT — full-seed visibility concerns reward under adversarial ordering; no cryptographic or security property is asserted. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT — independent samples support online selection under unknown distributions; no learning model, training procedure, or prediction guarantee is supplied. |
| optimisation_operations_research | True | strong | adaptable | inferred | The objective is online maximization of total nonnegative value subject to known matroid independence, compared with the best feasible choice after all values are known. The guarantee can support an allocation model only after its constraints and sampling interface satisfy the stated hypotheses. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT — no distributed execution, communication, failure, or consistency contract is provided. |
| other (Finite matroid combinatorics and probability) | True | strong | adaptable | stated | Finite matroid combinatorics and probability supply monotone density expansions, generator budgets, adaptive disjoint-query certificate bounds, and marked-pivot counting that support the online guarantee. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L1** (hypothesis): Feasibility must be a known finite labeled matroid; arbitrary resource constraints are outside the stated guarantee. Blocked use: ['Transfer to constraints without matroid independence and augmentation.']
- **L2** (hypothesis): Samples and online values must be mutually independent, nonnegative, and have matching laws at each label. Blocked use: ['Correlated sample or value coordinates.', 'Samples drawn from different laws than the corresponding online values.', 'Signed-value objectives.']
- **L3** (hypothesis): The prophet guarantee assumes a finite expected offline optimum. Blocked use: ['Applying the stated finite-expectation comparison when the expected optimum diverges.']
- **L4** (hypothesis): The complete initial seed is independent of samples and online values. Blocked use: ['Selecting values or distributions in response to the realized rule seed.']
- **L5** (hypothesis): Decisions are irrevocable and use only the available arrival history; feasibility must hold at every prefix. Blocked use: ['Batch selection using unseen future values.', 'Revision of earlier acceptances to improve the reward.']
- **L6** (counterexample_scope): The fixed-vector guarantee places the minimum over orders inside the expectation; a bound for each independently fixed order is insufficient for full-seed order selection. Blocked use: ['Replacing an expected minimum of rewards with a minimum of expected rewards.']
- **L7** (unknown): A finite measurable construction is supplied, but running time and polynomial independence-oracle complexity are ABSENT. Blocked use: ['Polynomial-time implementation claims.', 'Latency or practical scalability guarantees.']
- **L8** (approximation): The guarantee is an expected reward fraction relative to the offline matroid optimum, rather than a pathwise reward fraction. Blocked use: ['A reward guarantee for every realization of samples, values, and seed.', 'Claiming optimal reward on each execution.']
- **L9** (edge_case): Empty ground sets, loops, rank zero, ties, and zero values are explicitly covered. Loop values require no separate moment condition. Blocked use: ['Excluding stated degenerate cases from the rule.', 'Accepting loops or treating their values as selectable benchmark mass.']
- **L10** (hypothesis): Secretary weights are fixed before rule and arrival randomness, and the adversary cannot change the random-prefix law. Blocked use: ['Weights chosen after observing the seed or prefix.', 'Adversarial alteration of the observation-prefix distribution.']
- **L11** (hypothesis): The secretary result uses a random rejected observation prefix; only the remaining suffix can be rearranged adversarially. Blocked use: ['Applying the secretary consequence without its random observation prefix.']
- **L12** (formal_scope): The argument applies to nonrepresentable matroids and relies on rank and closure; a matrix representation is not a stated prerequisite. Blocked use: ['Restricting the mathematical theorem to representable matroids.', 'Assuming access through arrived elements alone suffices for this construction.']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 111 (`lean/docs/111.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [One Sample Suffices for Matroid Prophet Inequalities against an Almighty Adversary](../../preprints/One-Sample-Suffices-for-Matroid-Prophet-Inequalities-against-an-Almighty-Adversary-September-23-2026/final.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The formalization proves a one-sample matroid prophet inequality with expected reward at least $2^{-310}$ times the expected offline optimum. Each element has one independent sample paired with an identically distributed nonnegative online value, and all coordinates are independent. The rule and finite seed law depend only on the labeled matroid; choices are irrevocable and feasible after every prefix. The arrival order may depend measurably on all samples, values, and the seed, and only the offline optimum must be integrable.
>
> A supporting hidden-vector selection result for fixed nonnegative matroid weights gives expected worst-order reward at least $2^{-293}$ times the optimum, again with a finite seed law and prefix feasibility.

Comparator source `MatroidProphet.lean` (4003 bytes, sha256 `2bdb8a904832c5bc8e380d85951cca6f7cf535fc0c882b2b41384c2197dcadfa`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

/-!
# One-sample matroid selection

The rule and its finite seed law depend only on the known labeled matroid.
A decision sees the initial sample vector, the entire seed, and the arriving
label/value prefix. Every prefix is feasible, and decisions are irrevocable.
The arrival permutation may depend on all samples, values, and seed bits.
Only the actual offline optimum is required to be integrable.
-/

open MeasureTheory ProbabilityTheory
open scoped BigOperators

namespace MatroidProphet

abbrev Weights (n : ℕ) := Fin n → ℝ
abbrev Seed (bits : ℕ) := Fin bits → Bool
abbrev ArrivalOrder (n : ℕ) := Equiv.Perm (Fin n)
instance arrivalOrderMeasurableSpace (n : ℕ) : MeasurableSpace (ArrivalOrder n) := ⊤

abbrev History (n : ℕ) (k : Fin n) := Fin (k.val + 1) → Fin n × ℝ

structure OnlineRule (n bits : ℕ) where
  decide : (k : Fin n) → Seed bits → Weights n → History n k → Bool
  measurable_decide : ∀ k, Measurable
    (fun x : Seed bits × (Weights n × History n k) => decide k x.1 x.2.1 x.2.2)

def prefixIndex {n : ℕ} (k : Fin n) (j : Fin (k.val + 1)) : Fin n :=
  ⟨j.val, Nat.lt_of_lt_of_le j.isLt (Nat.succ_le_of_lt k.isLt)⟩

def history {n : ℕ} (v : Weights n) (π : ArrivalOrder n) (k : Fin n) : History n k :=
  fun j => let e := π (prefixIndex k j); (e, v e)

def decisionAt {n bits : ℕ} (A : OnlineRule n bits) (r : Seed bits)
    (s v : Weights n) (π : ArrivalOrder n) (k : Fin n) : Bool :=
  A.decide k r s (history v π k)

noncomputable def acceptedThrough {n bits : ℕ} (A : OnlineRule n bits) (r : Seed bits)
    (s v : Weights n) (π : ArrivalOrder n) (t : ℕ) : Finset (Fin n) := by
  classical
  exact Finset.univ.filter fun e =>
    (π.symm e).val < t ∧ decisionAt A r s v π (π.symm e) = true

noncomputable def accepted {n bits : ℕ} (A : OnlineRule n bits) (r : Seed bits)
    (s v : Weights n) (π : ArrivalOrder n) : Finset (Fin n) :=
  acceptedThrough A r s v π n

noncomputable def reward {n bits : ℕ} (A : OnlineRule n bits) (r : Seed bits)
    (s v : Weights n) (π : ArrivalOrder n) : ℝ :=
  ∑ e ∈ accepted A r s v π, v e

noncomputable def optimum {n : ℕ} (M : Matroid (Fin n)) (v : Weights n) : ℝ := by
  classical
  exact Finset.univ.sup' Finset.univ_nonempty
    (fun I : Finset (Fin n) => if M.Indep (I : Set (Fin n)) then ∑ e ∈ I, v e else 0)

def Feasible {n bits : ℕ} (M : Matroid (Fin n)) (A : OnlineRule n bits) : Prop :=
  ∀ (r : Seed bits) (s v : Weights n) (π : ArrivalOrder n) (t : ℕ),
    (∀ e, 0 ≤ s e) → (∀ e, 0 ≤ v e) →
    M.Indep (acceptedThrough A r s v π t : Set (Fin n))

def pairedCoordinates.{u} {n : ℕ} {Ω : Type u} (S V : Ω → Weights n)
    (i : Fin n × Bool) : Ω → ℝ :=
  if i.2 then fun ω => S ω i.1 else fun ω => V ω i.1

def OneSampleChallenge.{u} : Prop :=
  ∀ (n : ℕ) (M : Matroid (Fin n)), M.E = Set.univ →
    ∃ (bits : ℕ) (ν : Measure (Seed bits)), IsProbabilityMeasure ν ∧
    ∃ (A : OnlineRule n bits), Feasible M A ∧
      ∀ {Ω : Type u} [MeasurableSpace Ω] (μ : Measure Ω) [IsProbabilityMeasure μ]
        (S V : Ω → Weights n) (R : Ω → Seed bits),
        Measurable S → Measurable V → Measurable R →
        (∀ᵐ ω ∂μ, ∀ e, 0 ≤ S ω e) →
        (∀ᵐ ω ∂μ, ∀ e, 0 ≤ V ω e) →
        iIndepFun (pairedCoordinates S V) μ →
        (∀ e, Measure.map (fun ω => S ω e) μ = Measure.map (fun ω => V ω e) μ) →
        IndepFun (fun ω => (S ω, V ω)) R μ →
        Measure.map R μ = ν →
        Integrable (fun ω => optimum M (V ω)) μ →
        ∀ (π : Ω → ArrivalOrder n), Measurable π →
          Integrable (fun ω => reward A (R ω) (S ω) (V ω) (π ω)) μ ∧
          ((2 : ℝ) ^ 310)⁻¹ * (∫ ω, optimum M (V ω) ∂μ) ≤
            ∫ ω, reward A (R ω) (S ω) (V ω) (π ω) ∂μ

theorem one_sample.{u} : OneSampleChallenge.{u} := by
  sorry

end MatroidProphet

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that one independent sample per element suffices for a constant-competitive prophet inequality on every finite matroid. The guarantee holds even when the arrival-order adversary observes all samples, all online values, and the algorithm's entire random seed. The rule needs no description of the value distributions and achieves the absolute competitive ratio $`2^{-310}`$.

<a id="pm-h-2"></a>
### Introduction

A prophet inequality compares irrevocable online choices with the best feasible choice after all values are known. Here feasibility is described by a known finite labeled matroid $M=(E,\mathcal I)$: its independent sets $\mathcal I$ are closed under taking subsets and satisfy the augmentation axiom. For a nonnegative vector $v=(v_e:e\in E)$, write $$\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_M(v)=\max_{I\in\mathcal I}\sum_{e\in I}v_e.$$ An online rule observes labels and their values one at a time, and must accept or reject each label before the next arrival while keeping its accepted set independent. The value at each label has an unknown distribution; before arrivals the rule receives one independent sample from each of these distributions.

We prove a constant guarantee against an *almighty* ordering adversary. Besides the samples and the entire online value vector, this adversary sees the rule’s complete random seed before choosing the permutation. The rule is not told that future order. This exposes both the statistical information and every random choice that might otherwise protect the online rule from an unfavorable ordering.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There is a measurable online selection rule, independent of the value distributions, with the following property. Let $M=(E,\mathcal I)$ be any finite known labeled matroid, and let $(S_e,V_e:e\in E)$ be mutually independent nonnegative real random variables, with $S_e$ and $V_e$ having the same arbitrary law $D_e$. Assume $\mathbb E[\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_M(V)]<\infty$. The rule receives exactly the sample vector $S$ before arrivals and uses a seed $R$ independent of $S,V$.*

*For every measurable permutation rule $\pi$ depending on $M$, the laws $(D_e)$, $S$, $V$, and $R$, the rule accepts an independent set $A$, irrevocably deciding at each arrival using only the available history, and <a id="eq:main"></a>$$\label{eq:main}
 \mathbb E\left[\sum_{e\in A}V_e\right]
       \ge 2^{-310}\mathbb E[\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_M(V)].$$*

</div>

The constant is independent of the matroid, its size and rank, and the distributions. The rule is a finite measurable construction; no running-time or polynomial independence-oracle guarantee is claimed. Section <a href="#sec:accounting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:accounting">6</a> proves the stronger constant $2^{-293}$, leaving slack in the stated bound. We use no distributional description, extra samples, or randomness concealed from the adversary.

The proof also gives a secretary consequence. In that model the weights are fixed before any randomness, and the rule observes and rejects a random initial segment of a uniformly random permutation. After this prefix, an adversary that sees all weights, the ordered prefix, and the complete rule seed may rearrange the remaining labels arbitrarily. Corollary <a href="#cor:secretary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:secretary">18</a> gives a constant guarantee even with this suffix order. Both results follow from the same fixed-weight guarantee: after a random set of weights is revealed and its labels sacrificed, the expected minimum reward over all orders is a constant fraction of the fixed optimum.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
###### Historical context.

In the classical single-choice problem, independent nonnegative values with known distributions admit a sharp reward fraction of $1/2$; the original work of Krengel and Sucheston credits Garling for the factor-two bound (Krengel and Sucheston 1977) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[KS77](#ref-KS77)]. Kleinberg and Weinberg proved the same $1/2$ guarantee for arbitrary matroid constraints when the distributions are known (Kleinberg and Weinberg 2012) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[KW12](#ref-KW12)]. Their arrival model permits the next label to depend on previously revealed values, without giving the adversary the unseen values.

For unknown distributions, independent labeled samples provide a natural alternative source of information. Azar, Kleinberg, and Weinberg developed this limited-information framework and a reduction from order-oblivious secretary algorithms to single-sample prophet algorithms (Azar, Kleinberg, and Weinberg 2014) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[AKW14](#ref-AKW14)]. In the single-choice setting, Rubinstein, Wang, and Weinberg obtained the optimal one-sample reward fraction $1/2$ (Rubinstein, Wang, and Weinberg 2020) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[RWW20](#ref-RWW20)]. Caramanis et al. developed greedy-ordered selection and a pointwise sample-pair framework for single-sample guarantees under structured feasibility constraints (Caramanis et al. 2022) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[CDF22](#ref-CDF22)]. These works illustrate how symmetry between a sample and a realized value can replace knowledge of a distribution.

This connection also links sample-based selection to the matroid secretary problem, introduced by Babaioff, Immorlica, and Kleinberg (Babaioff, Immorlica, and Kleinberg 2007) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[BIK07](#ref-BIK07)]. In that problem the weights are fixed and the arrival order is uniformly random. Lachish (Lachish 2014) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Lachish14](#ref-Lachish14)] and Feldman, Svensson, and Zenklusen (Feldman, Svensson, and Zenklusen 2015) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[FSZ15](#ref-FSZ15)] obtained guarantees with a doubly logarithmic loss in the rank. The latter gave an order-oblivious rule, whose guarantee survives adversarial reordering after its observation prefix, and derived a one-sample prophet inequality with the same loss (Feldman, Svensson, and Zenklusen 2015, Theorem 1.1 and Corollary 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[FSZ15](#ref-FSZ15)]. Thus one sample already gave guarantees for general matroids; we obtain a constant independent of rank under full-seed order selection. Recent work also obtains constant guarantees for ordinary random-order matroid secretary selection (Singla 2026; Huang 2026) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Singla26](#ref-Singla26), [Huang26](#ref-Huang26)]. Section <a href="#sec:secretary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:secretary">7</a> compares these results, which retain uniformly random arrivals throughout, with the full-seed adversarial-suffix consequence proved here.

For every fixed $0<\delta<1/4$, Fu et al. obtained a reward fraction $1/4-\delta$ for arbitrary matroids on $n$ elements, using $O_\delta(\log^4(2+n))$ samples per element (Fu et al. 2024) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[FLTWWZ24](#ref-FLTWWZ24)]. Feldman, Svensson, and Zenklusen subsequently improved the sample dependence on the ground-set size and rank (Feldman, Svensson, and Zenklusen 2026) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[FSZ26](#ref-FSZ26)]. These latter results allow an almighty adversary, which observes all random realizations before choosing the order, including the algorithm’s randomness. The full-seed adversary is the almighty adversary of the greedy online-contention-resolution framework of Feldman, Svensson, and Zenklusen (Feldman, Svensson, and Zenklusen 2016, sec. 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[FSZ16](#ref-FSZ16)]. The distinction between one sample and a sample budget growing with the matroid remains essential even when the latter gives a much larger numerical constant.

For a different arrival model, Abdi, Banihashem, Hajiaghayi, and Mittal give a one-sample $1/2$ matroid prophet guarantee using $O(n^2)$ independence queries (Abdi et al. 2026, Theorem 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[ABHM26](#ref-ABHM26)]. Their order is fixed independently of the samples, online values, and internal random coins. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> instead permits the order to depend jointly on all of them. A bound for every fixed order controls the minimum of expected rewards, whereas this information pattern requires an expected minimum of rewards. The two guarantees therefore have different adversary interfaces, as well as different quantitative and computational bounds.

The density decompositions used below have a classical foundation in Edmonds’s matroid partition theorem (Edmonds 1965) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Edmonds65](#ref-Edmonds65)]. Soto used principal partitions and their uniformly dense minors for secretary selection in the random-assignment model (Soto 2013) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Soto13](#ref-Soto13)]; Santiago, Sergeev, and Zenklusen subsequently developed that approach without advance knowledge of the matroid (Santiago, Sergeev, and Zenklusen 2025) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[SSZ25](#ref-SSZ25)]. Our density optimizer is applied within each sampled weight group and incorporated into paths expanded by independent guards. The layer construction also has a predecessor in the alternating restriction–contraction minors along sampled spans used by Feldman, Svensson, and Zenklusen (Feldman, Svensson, and Zenklusen 2015, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[FSZ15](#ref-FSZ15)]. Here the sampled density expansions and guards produce the flats, and the analysis must control the expected minimum over orders after all seed information is exposed.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
###### Proof strategy.

We work first with a fixed hidden vector of weights. Independent masks reveal some coordinates, which are then sacrificed. The goal in this formulation is the expectation of the *minimum over all orders*, not a guarantee for an order chosen independently of the masks. A simple coordinatewise coupling subsequently transfers this guarantee to the one-sample model (Section <a href="#sec:setup" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:setup">2</a>).

After rounding weights into geometric levels, the main rule constructs nested paths of flats, that is, subsets closed under matroid span. The path index is an auxiliary integer, unrelated to arrival time. Process weight groups from low to high. For each group, enlarge the incoming flats to capture revealed labels at high density relative to rank. A separate sparse mask of revealed labels supplies *guards*, which enlarge a flat one auxiliary step before those labels enter its nominal path. Differences between alternate flats give two-step layers. Within each layer the rule greedily selects labels independent over the earlier endpoint flat. Nesting makes their union feasible for every arrival order (Section <a href="#sec:algorithm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:algorithm">3</a>).

The proof must show that these layers retain enough valuable rank. Three losses arise, each requiring a different argument.

First, lower-weight arrivals may block a focal weight group. At a revealed label’s nominal entry step, the *safety test* asks whether it remains outside the density expansion of the earlier flat together with all possible lower-weight competitors. Expose the guards backwards in auxiliary time, so that the nominal flats shrink. A disjoint-certificate bound shows that a label usually leaves at a step with substantial conditional exit probability. A second transition, using the independent mask that governs online eligibility, turns this exit estimate into an expected safe count (Section <a href="#sec:safety" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:safety">4</a>). All masks are drawn initially; reverse exposure analyzes their law without concealing any coins from the adversary.

Second, the safe labels themselves have been sacrificed. In each layer, choose an independent set spanning the unsacrificed labels over the earlier flat and lower-weight competitors. The density inequality bounds the safe count by the density charge times the total size of these sets, plus the number of labels left outside their span. A large safe count can therefore fail to yield rank only if this residual is large. The residual lies entirely in the sacrificed mask. Such a set can depend on that mask, so a bound for one fixed set does not suffice. We encode residuals by the marks at which their labels first become spanned in two sequences with boundedly many inserted generators. A combinatorial bound controls all such marked-pivot patterns before the focal group’s mask is exposed. Crucially, its bound depends on the number of inserted generators, not the number of auxiliary times. A union bound then rules out large residuals with high probability, giving rank on unsacrificed labels (Section <a href="#sec:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:transfer">5</a>).

Third, passing from a group’s nominal path to the final guarded path enlarges the layer bases, which can reduce that rank. One common set of generators accounts for all such expansions. A telescoping conditional-rank identity charges its cost only once across the entire path. After independent eligibility thinning, the remaining rank lower-bounds cumulative accepted counts simultaneously for every permutation. Only then do we average and sum over weight levels (Section <a href="#sec:accounting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:accounting">6</a>). A separate single-choice branch covers the case in which the heaviest label is a substantial part of the optimum.

Section <a href="#sec:secretary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:secretary">7</a> realizes the sacrificed mask as a random observation prefix and proves the secretary consequence.

<a id="sec:setup"></a>
### A fixed-vector formulation

Throughout, $M=(E,\mathcal I)$ is a finite labeled matroid. For $X\subseteq E$, its rank $\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(X)$ is the maximum size of an independent subset of $X$, and $\mathop{\mathrm{cl}}(X)=\{e\in E:\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(X\cup\{e\})=\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(X)\}$ is its closure. A flat equals its closure; $X$ spans $A$ when $A\subseteq\mathop{\mathrm{cl}}(X)$. Write $L=\mathop{\mathrm{cl}}(\varnothing)$ for the set of loops, and put $$\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(A\mid P)=\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(A\cup P)-\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(P),\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_M(w)=\max_{I\in\mathcal I}\sum_{e\in I}w_e.$$ Inside a closure, a rank argument, or a map on flats, a sum of sets denotes their union. A set is independent over $P$ if its conditional rank over $P$ equals its cardinality. We fix a total order on the labels once and for all.

We first consider a deterministic nonnegative weight vector $w$ that is initially hidden. Before arrivals, a rule draws a mask $B_0\subseteq E$ independently of $w$ and learns $w$ on $B_0$. It must reject every label in $B_0$. On other labels it learns the weight at arrival. The mask may be a function of the rule’s complete initial random seed $R$. Let $A(w,R,\pi)$ denote its accepted set under permutation $\pi$.

<a id="stmt-prop-hidden"></a>
<div id="prop:hidden" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:hidden-guarantee"></a>
**Proposition 2**. *There is a measurable rule in the fixed-vector formulation that is feasible for every realization and every permutation, and for every $w\in[0,\infty)^E$ satisfies $$\label{eq:hidden-guarantee}
 \mathbb E_R\left[\min_{\pi}\sum_{e\in A(w,R,\pi)}w_e\right]
 \ge 2^{-293}\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_M(w).$$ Its revealed mask is selected before any weights are seen.*

</div>

The minimum in <a href="#eq:hidden-guarantee" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hidden-guarantee">[eq:hidden-guarantee]</a> is inside the expectation. In particular, an adversary may choose a different order for each seed. All estimates below are for a fixed $M,w$; we suppress these deterministic objects in conditional expectations.

Using samples on a sacrificed observation set and realized values on its complement is the simulation underlying the limited-information reduction of Azar, Kleinberg, and Weinberg (Azar, Kleinberg, and Weinberg 2014, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[AKW14](#ref-AKW14)]. Related sample/value symmetries appear in the paired-draw analyses of Rubinstein, Wang, and Weinberg (Rubinstein, Wang, and Weinberg 2020, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[RWW20](#ref-RWW20)] and Caramanis et al. (Caramanis et al. 2022, sec. 2 and 6) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[CDF22](#ref-CDF22)]. Here the mask may depend on the entire seed, and the transfer must preserve the minimum inside the expectation. We verify these requirements directly.

<a id="stmt-lem-simulation"></a>
<div id="lem:simulation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (One-sample simulation). *A measurable fixed-vector rule satisfying <a href="#eq:hidden-guarantee" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hidden-guarantee">[eq:hidden-guarantee]</a> yields a measurable one-sample rule with the same competitive ratio against the adversary of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-simulation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-simulation</summary>

*Proof.* Draw its entire seed independently of the sample and value vectors $S,V$. For analysis, define $$w_e^{\mathrm{glue}}=
 \begin{cases}S_e,&e\in B_0(R),\\ V_e,&e\notin B_0(R).\end{cases}$$ Given any seed, the choices of which copies to use are fixed. The mutual independence and matching laws of $S_e,V_e$ imply $$\mathcal L(w^{\mathrm{glue}}\mid R)
       =\bigotimes_{e\in E}D_e.$$ Thus $w^{\mathrm{glue}}$ is independent of the complete seed and has the law of $V$. Implement the fixed-vector rule using $S_e$ only on its revealed mask and $V_e$ elsewhere when a label arrives. Ignore unused samples and reject revealed labels without using their online values. For every fixed $S,V,R$ and every permutation, induction on arrivals shows that this execution has exactly the accepted set $A(w^{\mathrm{glue}},R,\pi)$; each accepted weight is its actual online value.

Consequently, for any adversarial choice of $\pi$, including one depending on all the unused samples and values, its reward is at least $\min_{\pi'}\sum_{e\in A(w^{\mathrm{glue}},R,\pi')}w_e^{\mathrm{glue}}$. Integrate <a href="#eq:hidden-guarantee" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hidden-guarantee">[eq:hidden-guarantee]</a> using independence of the glued vector and seed. This gives the desired inequality. Nonnegative integration is valid without further moment assumptions, and feasibility bounds the reward by $\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_M(V)$, whose expectation is finite. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Elementary matroid facts

<a id="eq:rank-submodular"></a>
The independent sets contain $\varnothing$, are closed under subsets, and satisfy augmentation: for $I,J\in\mathcal I$ with $|I|<|J|$, some $e\in J\setminus I$ has $I\cup\{e\}\in\mathcal I$. A basis of a set $X$ is a maximal independent subset of $X$. We use only rank and closure, so the argument applies to nonrepresentable matroids as well. The augmentation axiom implies that all maximal independent subsets of a given set have the same size, and that independent sets extend to bases of larger sets. Rank is submodular: $$\label{eq:rank-submodular}
 \mathop{\mathrm{r}}(A)+\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(B)\ge\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(A\cup B)+\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(A\cap B).$$ Indeed, extend a basis of $A\cap B$ to one of $A$, then to one of $A\cup B$ using elements of $B\setminus A$. The initial intersection basis together with these last elements is independent in $B$, which proves the inequality. It follows that $\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(Z\mid P)$ decreases when $P$ grows. Closure is increasing and idempotent, and adjoining spanned elements changes neither rank nor closure. In particular, scanning a set and greedily accepting elements that increase rank over a fixed base $P$ selects an independent set over $P$ of size $\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(Z\mid P)$ and spans all of $Z$ over $P$.

<a id="sec:algorithm"></a>
### Constructing the fixed-vector rule

We construct the rule of Proposition <a href="#prop:hidden" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:hidden">2</a> for a fixed hidden weight vector. It uses geometric rounding and chooses with equal probability between a single-choice maximum branch and a main branch. The maximum branch handles weight concentrated on one label. The main branch filters arriving labels against revealed weights and assigns eligible arrivals to layers of nested flats constructed from those revealed weights. Greedy independent sets from different layers can then be combined feasibly.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Rounding and a maximum branch

<a id="eq:constants"></a>
<a id="eq:rounding"></a>
Fix the constants $$\label{eq:constants}
 B=2^{32},\qquad \kappa=2^{100},\qquad t=2^{-140},\qquad
 \eta=2^{-12},\qquad \gamma=\eta/16=2^{-16}.$$ Here $B$ separates weight levels, $\kappa$ is the rank charge in the density expansions, and $t$ is the common sparsity of the guard and eligibility masks. The exit-probability cutoff $\eta$ gives the sample-to-rank coefficient $\gamma$. The choices leave room for the losses combined in Section <a href="#sec:accounting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:accounting">6</a>. For $w_e>0$, round down to $u_e=B^{\lfloor\log_B w_e\rfloor}$, and put $u_e=0$ when $w_e=0$. Define $u(Z)=\sum_{e\in Z}u_e$ and $W=\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_M(u)$. The rule rounds each coordinate when its weight is revealed or arrives; the full vector $u$ is used only for analysis. Then $$\label{eq:rounding}
 \mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_M(w)/B\le W\le\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_M(w).$$ Larger rounded weights have higher priority, with ties resolved by the fixed label order. Priority greedy on all positive labels produces an independent set $I^*$ of weight $W$: its intersection with every initial segment of weight levels has full rank there, so summing the differences of successive levels proves optimality.

<a id="eq:maximum"></a>
Let $m_*$ be the largest rounded weight of a positive nonloop, or zero if there is none. A *maximum rule* reveals a fair independent mask and accepts the first unmasked positive nonloop of higher priority than every revealed positive nonloop, then accepts nothing further. If there are at least two positive nonloops, with probability $1/4$ the highest-priority one is unmasked and the second is masked. On this event only the highest can exceed the threshold, so it is accepted in every order. With one positive nonloop, success has probability $1/2$. Thus in all cases $$\label{eq:maximum}
 \mathbb E\big[\min_\pi u(A_{\mathrm{max}}(R,\pi))\big]\ge m_*/4.$$ The final fixed-vector rule uses this rule or the main rule constructed next, with equal probability. The maximum branch gives the guarantee when the largest rounded weight is large enough relative to the rounded optimum; the main branch handles the complementary case. Draw all coins, including unused branch coins, initially and independently of the weights.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Candidates and weight groups

For the main rule draw mutually independent masks $H,D,C,T$, independently across labels, with respective membership probabilities $1/2,1/4,t,t$. Their roles are summarized below.

<div class="center">

| Mask | Probability | Role                                                |
|:----:|:-----------:|:----------------------------------------------------|
| $H$  |    $1/2$    | Filters candidates by higher-priority span          |
| $D$  |    $1/4$    | Lists weight groups and supplies density data       |
| $C$  |     $t$     | Supplies guards for the flat paths                  |
| $T$  |     $t$     | Thins online eligibility independently of the paths |

</div>

Reveal the fixed-vector weights on $H\cup D\cup C$ and sacrifice these labels. Under Lemma <a href="#lem:simulation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:simulation">3</a>, these revealed weights are the corresponding sample coordinates. Membership in $T$ alone reveals no weight and forces no rejection. For each label $e$, let $H_{>e}$ be the labels in $H$ with higher priority than $e$. Define the candidate set and a comparison independent set by <a id="eq:candidates"></a>$$\label{eq:candidates}
 U=\{e\notin H:u_e>0,\ e\notin\mathop{\mathrm{cl}}(H_{>e})\},
 \qquad Y=I^*\setminus H.$$ The full set $U$ is used for analysis; the candidate test itself is computable as soon as the weight of $e$ is known.

<a id="stmt-lem-filter"></a>
<div id="lem:filter" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Candidate mass). *The set $Y$ is independent, $Y\subseteq U$, and <a id="eq:candidate-mass"></a>$$\label{eq:candidate-mass}
 \mathbb E_H u(Y)=W/2,\qquad \mathbb E_H u(U)\le W.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-filter"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-filter</summary>

*Proof.* For $e\in I^*$, no higher-priority labels span $e$, so neither do the higher-priority labels of $H$. This proves $Y\subseteq U$; its expectation follows from the fair mask. Conditional on all higher-priority memberships in $H$, the event $e\notin\mathop{\mathrm{cl}}(H_{>e})$ is fixed. Its contribution to $u(U)$ has the same expected weight as the event that $e$ belongs to $H$ and is selected by priority greedy on $H$. Sum over the positive labels. The resulting expected greedy weight on $H$ is at most $W$. ◻

</details>

Conditional on $H$, the *true weight groups* $$U_i=\{e\in U:u_e=B^i\},\qquad n_i=|U_i|,\qquad Y_i=Y\cap U_i
 \quad(i\in\mathbb Z)$$ are fixed. Empty groups may be omitted. A group is *listed* when $D\cap U_i\ne\varnothing$. Enumerate the listed groups as $G_1,\ldots,G_m$ from lower to higher weight, and write $D_h=D\cap G_h$, $C_h=C\cap G_h$, and $T_h=T\cap G_h$. The complete $D_h,C_h$ are known before arrivals: every label of $D\cup C$ is revealed, and its candidate test uses only $H$ and its own weight. The algorithm needs neither the complete $G_h$ nor the true group sizes $n_i$. All candidates are nonloops.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Density expansions

<a id="eq:density-objective"></a>
For a listed group $h$ and a flat $P$, let $Q_h(P)$ be the largest flat maximizing $$\label{eq:density-objective}
 f_h(Z)=|D_h\cap Z|-\kappa\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(Z)
 \qquad\text{over flats }Z\supseteq P.$$ For a nonflat argument, take its closure first. The objective rewards the number of $D_h$ labels spanned and charges $\kappa$ per unit of rank. Comparison with a further extension bounds its additional sample count by $\kappa$ times its additional rank. A separate telescoping argument in Lemma <a href="#lem:generators" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:generators">8</a> bounds the generators used over the entire path.

This is a contracted, sample-restricted form of the cardinality-minus-rank maximization associated with principal partitions; see Santiago, Sergeev, and Zenklusen (Santiago, Sergeev, and Zenklusen 2025, sec. 3.1, Equation (1)) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[SSZ25](#ref-SSZ25)]. We prove the needed monotonicity and residual-rank bounds directly.

<a id="stmt-lem-density"></a>
<div id="lem:density" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:density-residual"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Density expansion). *The largest maximizer in <a href="#eq:density-objective" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:density-objective">[eq:density-objective]</a> exists, the map $Q_h$ is increasing, and $P\subseteq Q_h(P)$. If $Q=Q_h(P)$, then for every set $Z$, $$\label{eq:density-residual}
 |D_h\cap(\mathop{\mathrm{cl}}(Q+Z)\setminus Q)|\le\kappa\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(Z\mid Q).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-density"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-density</summary>

*Proof.* The function $f_h$ is supermodular on the lattice of flats: the counting term is supermodular under intersection and closed union, and the negative rank term is supermodular by <a href="#eq:rank-submodular" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rank-submodular">[eq:rank-submodular]</a>. There are finitely many flats. The closed union of any two maximizers containing $P$ is again a maximizer, so there is a largest one.

For $P\subseteq P'$, put $Q=Q_h(P)$ and $Q'=Q_h(P')$. The flat $Q\cap Q'$ is feasible at $P$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{cl}}(Q\cup Q')$ is feasible at $P'$. Optimality gives one inequality and supermodularity the reverse: $$f_h(Q\cap Q')+f_h(\mathop{\mathrm{cl}}(Q\cup Q'))
       \le f_h(Q)+f_h(Q')
       \le f_h(Q\cap Q')+f_h(\mathop{\mathrm{cl}}(Q\cup Q')).$$ Equality in the two optimality comparisons shows that $\mathop{\mathrm{cl}}(Q\cup Q')$ is also a maximizer at $P'$. Its largest maximizer contains $Q$, as claimed. Finally compare $Q$ with the feasible flat $\mathop{\mathrm{cl}}(Q+Z)$ in <a href="#eq:density-objective" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:density-objective">[eq:density-objective]</a>. The difference of their ranks is $\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(Z\mid Q)$, giving <a href="#eq:density-residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:density-residual">[eq:density-residual]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Nominal paths and guards

Integer $k$ denotes an *auxiliary time*, unrelated to arrival time. Set $F_0(k)=L$ for $k<0$ and $F_0(k)=E$ for $k\ge0$. For each listed group, put $a_h=-3h$ and define an enabled expansion $$Q_{h,k}(P)=
 \begin{cases}Q_h(P),&k\ge a_h,\\ P,&k<a_h,\end{cases}
 \qquad(P\text{ a flat}).$$ In increasing group order, form its *nominal* and *guarded* paths <a id="eq:paths"></a>$$\label{eq:paths}
 X_h(k)=Q_{h,k}(F_{h-1}(k)),\qquad
 F_h(k)=\mathop{\mathrm{cl}}\bigl(X_h(k)+(C_h\cap X_h(k+1))\bigr).$$ Thus a guard uses a label one auxiliary step before it enters the nominal path. Each density map uses only $D_h$ and the known matroid, and each guard set uses the revealed labels $C_h$. Consequently these flats can be computed as subsets of $E$ before arrivals, even though the weights of some labels they contain remain unknown.

<a id="stmt-lem-paths"></a>
<div id="lem:paths" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:path-inclusions"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Path properties). *The paths increase with $k$ and satisfy $$\label{eq:path-inclusions}
 F_{h-1}(k)\subseteq X_h(k)\subseteq F_h(k)\subseteq X_h(k+1).$$ They equal $E$ for $k\ge0$. Moreover $X_h(k)=L$ for $k<a_h$ and $F_h(k)=L$ for $k<a_h-1$. If $e$ is a nonloop, its nominal birth $$b_h(e)=\min\{k:e\in X_h(k)\}$$ is either $a_h$, with $e\in Q_h(L)$, or belongs to $[a_h+2,0]$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-paths"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-paths</summary>

*Proof.* Induct on $h$. The map $Q_{h,k}$ is increasing both in its argument and in $k$, since enabling it replaces the identity by an extensive increasing map. Thus $X_h$ and then $F_h$ are increasing. The first two inclusions in <a href="#eq:path-inclusions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-inclusions">[eq:path-inclusions]</a> are extensiveness; the last follows because both sets adjoined in the definition of $F_h(k)$ lie in the flat $X_h(k+1)$. The same recursion gives $E$ at times at least zero.

For $h>1$, $a_{h-1}=a_h+3$ and the inductive lower bound makes $F_{h-1}(k)=L$ for $k<a_h+2$; this also holds for $h=1$ directly from $F_0$. Before $a_h$ the new map is the identity, proving the asserted lower bounds for $X_h$ and $F_h$. At $a_h$ and $a_h+1$, the nominal flat is the same flat $Q_h(L)$, so no nonloop is born at $a_h+1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### The online decisions

Choose an independent fair parity $\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}$ and put $\widehat F(k)=F_m(k)$. When $m=0$, use $\widehat F=F_0$ and reject all arrivals. In general the layer endpoints are the integers $b\equiv\varepsilon\pmod2$, with layer $(b-2,b]$ having base $\widehat F(b-2)$. The rule maintains a set $A_b\subseteq\widehat F(b)$ in each layer, independent over its base $\widehat F(b-2)$. Since successive layers share an endpoint, all labels selected in an earlier layer lie in the base of every later layer. Greedy selection in alternating restriction–contraction minors also appears in Feldman, Svensson, and Zenklusen (Feldman, Svensson, and Zenklusen 2015, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[FSZ15](#ref-FSZ15)]; here the nested flats come from the guarded density paths.

At arrival of a label $e$, perform the following operations.

1.  Reject if $e\in H\cup D\cup C$, if its candidate test fails, or if its rounded weight is not listed.

2.  For its listed group $h$, compute $b=b_h(e)$. Reject unless <a id="eq:eligibility"></a>$$\label{eq:eligibility}
     e\in T,\qquad b\ge a_h+2,\qquad b\equiv\varepsilon\pmod2,
     \qquad e\notin\widehat F(b-2).$$

3.  Let $A_b$ be the previously accepted labels assigned to this layer. Accept $e$ exactly when $\mathop{\mathrm{r}}(\{e\}\mid\widehat F(b-2)+A_b)=1$, and then add it to $A_b$.

These operations use the revealed data, the arriving label and weight, the seed, and the previous decisions. In particular, no full true weight group or future arrival is needed. By Lemma <a href="#lem:paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:paths">6</a>, the three-step activation schedule leaves no birth at $a_h+1$. Excluding the baseline birth $a_h$ therefore leaves exactly the births for which $b-2\ge a_h$: the density map is already enabled at the preceding layer endpoint. Figure <a href="#fig:paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:paths">1</a> shows both the guard step and the nesting that makes layerwise decisions feasible.

<figure id="fig:paths">

<figcaption>Above: a density expansion followed by guards from the next nominal time gives $F_{h}(k) ⊆ X_{h}(k+1)$. Below: two consecutive layers of the chosen parity on the final path. The set $A_{b}$ is independent over $F̂(b−2)$ and lies in $F̂(b)$; $A_{b + 2}$ is independent over this latter base. In the lower panel, the horizontal order is auxiliary time, regardless of the actual arrival order.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-paths"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:paths`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex

 flat/.style={draw=linkblue,rounded corners=2pt,inner sep=7pt}]
 \node[flat] (prior) {$F_{h-1}(k)$};
 \node[flat,right=23mm of prior] (nominal) {$X_h(k)$};
 \node[flat,right=31mm of nominal] (guarded) {$F_h(k)$};
 \node[above=13mm of guarded] (guard) {$C_h\cap X_h(k+1)$};
 \draw[->] (prior) -- node[above] {$Q_{h,k}$} (nominal);
 \draw[->] (nominal) -- node[below] {adjoin and close} (guarded);
 \draw[->] (guard) -- (guarded);
 \node[flat,below=27mm of prior] (leftbase) {$\widehat F(b-2)$};
 \node[flat,below=27mm of nominal] (middlebase) {$\widehat F(b)$};
 \node[flat,below=27mm of guarded] (rightbase) {$\widehat F(b+2)$};
 \draw[->] (leftbase) -- node[above] {$\subseteq$}
     node[below=1mm,align=center] {layer $(b-2,b]$\\accept $A_b$} (middlebase);
 \draw[->] (middlebase) -- node[above] {$\subseteq$}
     node[below=1mm,align=center] {layer $(b,b+2]$\\accept $A_{b+2}$} (rightbase);
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 9 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):
